And the worst part is the sneaking around. I’ll come back down and just start wiping the counters or putting dishes away, acting as if I’m just tidying up the kitchen or getting ready for bed, pretending everything is perfectly normal while my husband is right there in the living room watching television. It’s just... it’s taking such a toll on me, the hiding and the pretending. I just feel so ashamed of it all.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating too much happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been going on for a while now, if I'm being completely honest. I remember it really starting to become a regular thing around last winter. The days were getting so short, and work was just piling up with all the end-of-year insurance benefits people were rushing to use for their glasses, and I would just come home completely drained. And since then, since last winter, I don't think there's been a single week where I haven't had at least one of these... these private eating spells. \n\nAnd it’s not just the eating, it’s what comes after. Because the panic sets in, you see? I look at all the wrappers or the empty plates, and I just think, \"What have I done?\" So, to fix it, to make up for all that food, I’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, like I mentioned. Or sometimes I’ll just completely skip meals the next day, telling my husband I had a huge lunch at the clinic and I'm just not hungry. Or I'll go out for a walk, but not just a normal stroll—I'll push myself to walk for miles and miles, long past the point where my feet are aching from standing all day at work, just trying to burn it all off. It’s like a punishment, really. But yes, it’s been at least once a week, every single week, since last winter. It's exhausting just thinking about the cycle of it all.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned going out for a walk to burn off calories, but do you ever engage in any other form of exercise to try to compensate for the food you've eaten?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, the walking is a big part of it, but it's not just the walking on my own. I actually belong to this local walking group—we usually meet up on Tuesday and Thursday mornings, and sometimes on the weekends if the weather is nice. It started out as just a social thing, you know, a way to get some fresh air and chat with some of the other ladies in the neighborhood before I have to head into the clinic and spend the rest of the day indoors under those awful fluorescent lights. \n\nBut lately, well, if I've had one of those episodes the night before, the walking group completely changes for me. It stops being about socializing or enjoying the morning. I'll show up, and while everyone else is just chatting and keeping a nice, steady pace, I'll push myself to do extra laps. I'll tell them I'm just feeling really energetic, or that I want to get my heart rate up, but the truth is, I'm not feeling energetic at all. I'm usually exhausted because I didn't sleep well from the guilt. But I just keep pushing myself, walking faster and farther than everyone else, practically speed-walking until I'm out of breath and my legs are burning. \n\nAnd on top of that, the next day, I'll just completely skip breakfast and lunch. I'll make my husband his coffee and toast, and I'll just have black coffee. Then at work, when everyone is ordering sandwiches or going to the breakroom, I'll just say I'm too busy with a complicated prescription or that I had a huge breakfast, which is a complete lie, of course. I just power through on an empty stomach, feeling lightheaded sometimes, but I tell myself I have to do it. It's not because I'm not hungry—I'm usually starving by noon—but I just feel like I have to undo what I did the night before. The extra laps, the starving all day... it's all just to try and erase the food, to make myself feel clean again, I suppose. It's just a constant, exhausting cycle of trying to make up for my mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask about my weight, because... well, other people seem to think I've lost quite a bit. My daughter, for instance, came over for Sunday dinner a few weeks ago, and she just kept looking at me, telling me I looked too thin, asking if I was feeling alright. And then there's a woman from my church—Mrs. Gable, she sits two pews behind us—she actually stopped me after service just to say I looked like I was wasting away, which I thought was a bit much, honestly. \n\nBut the thing is, Doctor, I just don't see it that way. I really don't. Even with them saying those things, if I put on a skirt for work and it feels even the slightest bit snug around the waist, or if I step on the scale in the morning—which I do every single morning, right after I shower—and the number is up even just one single pound, I absolutely panic. My heart starts racing, and my mind just spirals. It feels as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight, just from one bad evening. It’s this terrifying feeling that I'm just going to keep expanding, that all my clothes won't fit, and everyone at the clinic will notice. So, no matter what my daughter or Mrs. Gable says, I just live in this constant, exhausting fear of gaining weight. It's like I can't trust my own eyes when I look in the mirror, you know? I just see all the flaws and the potential to be heavy again.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to compensate for the food you've eaten during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, throwing up is... it's a big part of it, yes. I know I mentioned the walking, the extra laps with the group, and the starving myself the next day, which are all exhausting in their own right, but... the throwing up is really the thing that pushed me to finally come in and talk to someone. It’s what finally made me say something, because it’s just become this awful, secretive routine. \n\nAfter I eat too much, after one of those episodes where I just lose all control and the wrappers are everywhere, the panic is just so overwhelming. I can't even sit with myself. So I'll quietly gather everything up, throw the evidence deep in the trash can under some paper towels, and then I’ll head upstairs. I’ll go into the master bathroom, lock the door, and just... make myself throw up. And the whole time, my heart is pounding out of my chest because I'm terrified my husband will hear me. He usually watches his history documentaries in the living room right below us, and the sound travels, you know? \n\nSo after I do it, I have to spend another ten minutes washing my face, rinsing my mouth out with mouthwash, sometimes even brushing my teeth twice just to make sure there's no smell. And then I have to walk back downstairs and just... act normal. I'll pretend I was just up there getting ready for bed, putting on my night cream, or I'll go back to the kitchen and make a big show of wiping down the counters or running the dishwasher, acting as if I’m just tidying up for the night. I'll smile and ask him about his show, all while my throat is burning and my eyes are still watering. It's just... it's the lying, the sneaking around in my own home, pretending to be this put-together woman who just had a normal evening, when really I was just upstairs making myself sick. That's the part that really broke me down and made me realize I couldn't keep living like this. It's just so humiliating, having to put on that performance every single time.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to purge after eating too much?\n\nPatient: Well, no, throwing up isn't the only thing, though like I said, it's the part that really scared me into coming here. But, well, there are the laxatives, too. I didn't want to mention them because it's... it's just so embarrassing, honestly. But yes, I do use them. \n\nIt started, oh, maybe a year ago? I was at the pharmacy—not the one I work at, of course, I'd never buy them where my colleagues could see me. I drive a couple of towns over to this big chain pharmacy where nobody knows me. I'll go in and buy a box, usually the store brand because it's cheaper, and I'll bury it under a bunch of other things in my basket—like cotton balls, or a magazine, or some hand lotion I don't even need, just so the cashier doesn't think anything of it. \n\nAfter one of those terrible nights, if I feel like throwing up wasn't enough, or if I just feel so heavy and disgusting that I can't stand it, I'll take them. The box says to take one or two, but... well, I usually take four, sometimes five. I know it's terrible. I know it is. And the cramps... oh, the cramps the next day are just agonizing. I'll be at work, trying to fit a pair of progressive lenses for a patient, and I'll just get this sharp, twisting pain in my stomach. I have to clench my teeth and force a smile and pretend everything is perfectly fine, and then excuse myself to the restroom the second I can get away. It's miserable. I'll be in there sweating, terrified someone is going to come in and hear me, or that my manager will wonder why I'm taking so many breaks. \n\nAnd the worst part is, it doesn't even really make me feel better. It just makes me feel weak and dehydrated, and my skin gets so dry. But in my head, I just feel like I have to flush everything out of my system, like I have to completely empty myself to undo the damage. Between the walking, the starving, the throwing up, and the laxatives... it's like a full-time job just trying to manage it all. It's completely taken over my life, Doctor. I just don't know how to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly, Doctor. I know I was going on and on about this just a few minutes ago when you asked about my weight, but it really is just this endless, exhausting loop in my head. Like I said, I have my daughter and Mrs. Gable from church telling me I look too thin, and logically, I hear the words they're saying. I really do. But it just doesn't register in my brain the way it should. It's like they're speaking a completely different language. \n\nBecause the reality is, if I'm getting ready for work at the dispensary—and I have to look put-together, you know, fitting glasses means you're right up in people's personal space, they notice everything about you—if I pull on one of my work skirts and it feels even a fraction of an inch snug around the waist, the panic is just instantaneous. Or if I step on that scale in the morning and the digital number is up by just one single pound... I mean, it's not just a passing worry, it's a full-blown physical reaction. My chest tightens, my breathing gets shallow, and I genuinely feel as if I’ve somehow let myself become absolutely huge overnight. \n\nIt feels completely real to me in that moment, like I've just ballooned out of nowhere. It doesn't matter that my rational mind knows, scientifically, a person can't gain ten pounds of fat in one night. My mind just screams at me that I've ruined everything, that I'm huge now, and that everyone at the clinic is going to stare at me. So yes, to answer your question, it's a constant, everyday, all-consuming worry. I just can't seem to shake that absolute terror of getting bigger, no matter what my daughter or anyone else tells me about how I look.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes of eating too much without then trying to purge or compensate for it?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I don't think I've ever... no, I couldn't possibly. I mean, just the thought of doing that—of just sitting there with all that food inside me, feeling so horribly full, and doing absolutely nothing to fix it? It makes my skin crawl just hearing you ask about it. \n\nThere was this one time, maybe a few months ago, where I actually tried. I had eaten... oh, it was half a cherry pie and nearly a whole sleeve of those buttery crackers, the Ritz ones that leave crumbs absolutely everywhere, and I told myself, \"Just go to bed. Just lay down, close your eyes, and let it go.\" But I couldn't. I laid there in the dark, and my stomach felt so stretched and heavy, and my mind was just racing at a million miles an hour. I could hear my husband breathing next to me—he had come up to bed after his history documentaries finished—and all I could think about was how I had failed again, and how all that food was just sitting there inside me, ruining me. \n\nI think I lasted maybe twenty minutes, tossing and turning, before the panic got so bad I was practically vibrating. I just had to get up. I had to go to the bathroom and take care of it. It's like... it's like an alarm going off in my head that just won't stop ringing until I do something to undo the mistake. So, no, whether it's the throwing up, or taking those awful laxatives, or pacing around the neighborhood at five in the morning, or just completely refusing to eat a single thing the entire next day at the dispensary... I always, always have to do something. I have to pay for it, somehow. I just couldn't eat like that and then move on with my evening like a normal person. It's just completely impossible for my brain to allow that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat too much without then trying to purge or compensate for it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I... like I think I just mentioned a moment ago, it's really never. Zero. Not a single time. I know I just went on and on about that night with the cherry pie, but that really is the absolute truth of it—the frequency is just nonexistent. I have never once been able to just eat a large amount of food and then leave it alone. \n\nIt’s just... it's a completely unbreakable rule in my head at this point. If the eating happens, the fixing it *must* follow. There are no exceptions. I mean, even if I have a terrible head cold, or if I've been on my feet all day at the dispensary dealing with difficult patients who complain about their bifocal lines, and I come home and end up losing control in the pantry... no matter how bone-tired I am, I will still force myself to compensate for it. I’ll still drag myself to the bathroom, or I’ll still set my alarm for four in the morning to go walk in the freezing cold before my husband wakes up. \n\nSo, yes, to give you a direct answer to how often it happens, it never does. Not once. The idea of just having an episode like that and then sitting on the couch to watch television or read a book like a normal person... it’s completely foreign to me now. I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, it’s just that the panic is so deeply tied to the eating that the two things are basically glued together in my brain. You can't have one without the other. It's just always, always followed by some way to get rid of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doctor, I... I feel like we're going in circles here a little bit. I know you have your questions you have to get through, but I really, truly meant what I just said. Never. Not once. I have never, ever had an episode where I ate a huge amount of food and didn't feel that absolute, crushing need to do something about it. 

I mean, the need is the whole problem, isn't it? It's not just a passing thought of, "Oh, I shouldn't have eaten that." It's a physical compulsion. It's like... it's like if you were underwater and you needed to breathe. You wouldn't just sit there and think, "Well, I guess I'll just drown." You would thrash, you would fight, you would do whatever it took to get to the surface. That's what it feels like for me with the food. The second I realize I've eaten too much—whether it's half a pie or a whole box of cereal or whatever it is—that feeling of drowning just washes over me. 

And it's not just the throwing up, like I said. Sometimes it's the fasting. Sometimes I'll go into the dispensary the next day, and my stomach will be growling so loudly I'm terrified the patients can hear it while I'm adjusting their frames, but I won't let myself have even a single sip of juice. Not a cracker, not a mint. Nothing. Because the need to compensate is so strong, it overrides everything else. It overrides my hunger, it overrides my common sense, it overrides my exhaustion. 

So, no. To ask if I've ever eaten an excessive amount and *not* felt the need to purge or compensate... it's just impossible for me to even imagine. The eating and the panic, the eating and the fixing... they are exactly the same thing in my mind. There has never been a time where one happened without the other demanding to follow right behind it. Never. I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, but it really is just the absolute truth of my life right now.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello, Doctor. Thank you for seeing me. I know I probably should have come in sooner, but, you know, it's just so hard to find the time, especially with work. I'm a dispensing optician, and we've been just dreadfully short-staffed lately, so I'm always on my feet, adjusting frames, dealing with prescriptions, trying to keep everyone happy. It’s exhausting, really. But... well, the reason I finally made the appointment—and it took a lot of courage, I have to admit, because it's not something I'm proud of—is that things have gotten a bit out of hand at home. \n\nYou see, lately, well, I'll have these moments where I just eat... too much. Much more than I intend to. And then, the guilt sets in. It's this awful, heavy feeling, and I just can't stand it. So, what finally made me realize I need to say something to a professional is that after these episodes, I've been going upstairs to the bathroom and... well, making myself throw up. And the worst part is the sneaking around. I’ll come back down and just start wiping the counters or putting dishes away, acting as if I’m just tidying up the kitchen or getting ready for bed, pretending everything is perfectly normal while my husband is right there in the living room watching television. It’s just... it’s taking such a toll on me, the hiding and the pretending. I just feel so ashamed of it all.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating too much happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been going on for a while now, if I'm being completely honest. I remember it really starting to become a regular thing around last winter. The days were getting so short, and work was just piling up with all the end-of-year insurance benefits people were rushing to use for their glasses, and I would just come home completely drained. And since then, since last winter, I don't think there's been a single week where I haven't had at least one of these... these private eating spells. \n\nAnd it’s not just the eating, it’s what comes after. Because the panic sets in, you see? I look at all the wrappers or the empty plates, and I just think, \"What have I done?\" So, to fix it, to make up for all that food, I’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, like I mentioned. Or sometimes I’ll just completely skip meals the next day, telling my husband I had a huge lunch at the clinic and I'm just not hungry. Or I'll go out for a walk, but not just a normal stroll—I'll push myself to walk for miles and miles, long past the point where my feet are aching from standing all day at work, just trying to burn it all off. It’s like a punishment, really. But yes, it’s been at least once a week, every single week, since last winter. It's exhausting just thinking about the cycle of it all.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned going out for a walk to burn off calories, but do you ever engage in any other form of exercise to try to compensate for the food you've eaten?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, the walking is a big part of it, but it's not just the walking on my own. I actually belong to this local walking group—we usually meet up on Tuesday and Thursday mornings, and sometimes on the weekends if the weather is nice. It started out as just a social thing, you know, a way to get some fresh air and chat with some of the other ladies in the neighborhood before I have to head into the clinic and spend the rest of the day indoors under those awful fluorescent lights. \n\nBut lately, well, if I've had one of those episodes the night before, the walking group completely changes for me. It stops being about socializing or enjoying the morning. I'll show up, and while everyone else is just chatting and keeping a nice, steady pace, I'll push myself to do extra laps. I'll tell them I'm just feeling really energetic, or that I want to get my heart rate up, but the truth is, I'm not feeling energetic at all. I'm usually exhausted because I didn't sleep well from the guilt. But I just keep pushing myself, walking faster and farther than everyone else, practically speed-walking until I'm out of breath and my legs are burning. \n\nAnd on top of that, the next day, I'll just completely skip breakfast and lunch. I'll make my husband his coffee and toast, and I'll just have black coffee. Then at work, when everyone is ordering sandwiches or going to the breakroom, I'll just say I'm too busy with a complicated prescription or that I had a huge breakfast, which is a complete lie, of course. I just power through on an empty stomach, feeling lightheaded sometimes, but I tell myself I have to do it. It's not because I'm not hungry—I'm usually starving by noon—but I just feel like I have to undo what I did the night before. The extra laps, the starving all day... it's all just to try and erase the food, to make myself feel clean again, I suppose. It's just a constant, exhausting cycle of trying to make up for my mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask about my weight, because... well, other people seem to think I've lost quite a bit. My daughter, for instance, came over for Sunday dinner a few weeks ago, and she just kept looking at me, telling me I looked too thin, asking if I was feeling alright. And then there's a woman from my church—Mrs. Gable, she sits two pews behind us—she actually stopped me after service just to say I looked like I was wasting away, which I thought was a bit much, honestly. \n\nBut the thing is, Doctor, I just don't see it that way. I really don't. Even with them saying those things, if I put on a skirt for work and it feels even the slightest bit snug around the waist, or if I step on the scale in the morning—which I do every single morning, right after I shower—and the number is up even just one single pound, I absolutely panic. My heart starts racing, and my mind just spirals. It feels as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight, just from one bad evening. It’s this terrifying feeling that I'm just going to keep expanding, that all my clothes won't fit, and everyone at the clinic will notice. So, no matter what my daughter or Mrs. Gable says, I just live in this constant, exhausting fear of gaining weight. It's like I can't trust my own eyes when I look in the mirror, you know? I just see all the flaws and the potential to be heavy again.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to compensate for the food you've eaten during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, throwing up is... it's a big part of it, yes. I know I mentioned the walking, the extra laps with the group, and the starving myself the next day, which are all exhausting in their own right, but... the throwing up is really the thing that pushed me to finally come in and talk to someone. It’s what finally made me say something, because it’s just become this awful, secretive routine. \n\nAfter I eat too much, after one of those episodes where I just lose all control and the wrappers are everywhere, the panic is just so overwhelming. I can't even sit with myself. So I'll quietly gather everything up, throw the evidence deep in the trash can under some paper towels, and then I’ll head upstairs. I’ll go into the master bathroom, lock the door, and just... make myself throw up. And the whole time, my heart is pounding out of my chest because I'm terrified my husband will hear me. He usually watches his history documentaries in the living room right below us, and the sound travels, you know? \n\nSo after I do it, I have to spend another ten minutes washing my face, rinsing my mouth out with mouthwash, sometimes even brushing my teeth twice just to make sure there's no smell. And then I have to walk back downstairs and just... act normal. I'll pretend I was just up there getting ready for bed, putting on my night cream, or I'll go back to the kitchen and make a big show of wiping down the counters or running the dishwasher, acting as if I’m just tidying up for the night. I'll smile and ask him about his show, all while my throat is burning and my eyes are still watering. It's just... it's the lying, the sneaking around in my own home, pretending to be this put-together woman who just had a normal evening, when really I was just upstairs making myself sick. That's the part that really broke me down and made me realize I couldn't keep living like this. It's just so humiliating, having to put on that performance every single time.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to purge after eating too much?\n\nPatient: Well, no, throwing up isn't the only thing, though like I said, it's the part that really scared me into coming here. But, well, there are the laxatives, too. I didn't want to mention them because it's... it's just so embarrassing, honestly. But yes, I do use them. \n\nIt started, oh, maybe a year ago? I was at the pharmacy—not the one I work at, of course, I'd never buy them where my colleagues could see me. I drive a couple of towns over to this big chain pharmacy where nobody knows me. I'll go in and buy a box, usually the store brand because it's cheaper, and I'll bury it under a bunch of other things in my basket—like cotton balls, or a magazine, or some hand lotion I don't even need, just so the cashier doesn't think anything of it. \n\nAfter one of those terrible nights, if I feel like throwing up wasn't enough, or if I just feel so heavy and disgusting that I can't stand it, I'll take them. The box says to take one or two, but... well, I usually take four, sometimes five. I know it's terrible. I know it is. And the cramps... oh, the cramps the next day are just agonizing. I'll be at work, trying to fit a pair of progressive lenses for a patient, and I'll just get this sharp, twisting pain in my stomach. I have to clench my teeth and force a smile and pretend everything is perfectly fine, and then excuse myself to the restroom the second I can get away. It's miserable. I'll be in there sweating, terrified someone is going to come in and hear me, or that my manager will wonder why I'm taking so many breaks. \n\nAnd the worst part is, it doesn't even really make me feel better. It just makes me feel weak and dehydrated, and my skin gets so dry. But in my head, I just feel like I have to flush everything out of my system, like I have to completely empty myself to undo the damage. Between the walking, the starving, the throwing up, and the laxatives... it's like a full-time job just trying to manage it all. It's completely taken over my life, Doctor. I just don't know how to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly, Doctor. I know I was going on and on about this just a few minutes ago when you asked about my weight, but it really is just this endless, exhausting loop in my head. Like I said, I have my daughter and Mrs. Gable from church telling me I look too thin, and logically, I hear the words they're saying. I really do. But it just doesn't register in my brain the way it should. It's like they're speaking a completely different language. \n\nBecause the reality is, if I'm getting ready for work at the dispensary—and I have to look put-together, you know, fitting glasses means you're right up in people's personal space, they notice everything about you—if I pull on one of my work skirts and it feels even a fraction of an inch snug around the waist, the panic is just instantaneous. Or if I step on that scale in the morning and the digital number is up by just one single pound... I mean, it's not just a passing worry, it's a full-blown physical reaction. My chest tightens, my breathing gets shallow, and I genuinely feel as if I’ve somehow let myself become absolutely huge overnight. \n\nIt feels completely real to me in that moment, like I've just ballooned out of nowhere. It doesn't matter that my rational mind knows, scientifically, a person can't gain ten pounds of fat in one night. My mind just screams at me that I've ruined everything, that I'm huge now, and that everyone at the clinic is going to stare at me. So yes, to answer your question, it's a constant, everyday, all-consuming worry. I just can't seem to shake that absolute terror of getting bigger, no matter what my daughter or anyone else tells me about how I look.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes of eating too much without then trying to purge or compensate for it?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I don't think I've ever... no, I couldn't possibly. I mean, just the thought of doing that—of just sitting there with all that food inside me, feeling so horribly full, and doing absolutely nothing to fix it? It makes my skin crawl just hearing you ask about it. \n\nThere was this one time, maybe a few months ago, where I actually tried. I had eaten... oh, it was half a cherry pie and nearly a whole sleeve of those buttery crackers, the Ritz ones that leave crumbs absolutely everywhere, and I told myself, \"Just go to bed. Just lay down, close your eyes, and let it go.\" But I couldn't. I laid there in the dark, and my stomach felt so stretched and heavy, and my mind was just racing at a million miles an hour. I could hear my husband breathing next to me—he had come up to bed after his history documentaries finished—and all I could think about was how I had failed again, and how all that food was just sitting there inside me, ruining me. \n\nI think I lasted maybe twenty minutes, tossing and turning, before the panic got so bad I was practically vibrating. I just had to get up. I had to go to the bathroom and take care of it. It's like... it's like an alarm going off in my head that just won't stop ringing until I do something to undo the mistake. So, no, whether it's the throwing up, or taking those awful laxatives, or pacing around the neighborhood at five in the morning, or just completely refusing to eat a single thing the entire next day at the dispensary... I always, always have to do something. I have to pay for it, somehow. I just couldn't eat like that and then move on with my evening like a normal person. It's just completely impossible for my brain to allow that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat too much without then trying to purge or compensate for it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I... like I think I just mentioned a moment ago, it's really never. Zero. Not a single time. I know I just went on and on about that night with the cherry pie, but that really is the absolute truth of it—the frequency is just nonexistent. I have never once been able to just eat a large amount of food and then leave it alone. \n\nIt’s just... it's a completely unbreakable rule in my head at this point. If the eating happens, the fixing it *must* follow. There are no exceptions. I mean, even if I have a terrible head cold, or if I've been on my feet all day at the dispensary dealing with difficult patients who complain about their bifocal lines, and I come home and end up losing control in the pantry... no matter how bone-tired I am, I will still force myself to compensate for it. I’ll still drag myself to the bathroom, or I’ll still set my alarm for four in the morning to go walk in the freezing cold before my husband wakes up. \n\nSo, yes, to give you a direct answer to how often it happens, it never does. Not once. The idea of just having an episode like that and then sitting on the couch to watch television or read a book like a normal person... it’s completely foreign to me now. I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, it’s just that the panic is so deeply tied to the eating that the two things are basically glued together in my brain. You can't have one without the other. It's just always, always followed by some way to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate an excessive amount of food without feeling the need to purge or compensate for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I feel like we're going in circles here a little bit. I know you have your questions you have to get through, but I really, truly meant what I just said. Never. Not once. I have never, ever had an episode where I ate a huge amount of food and didn't feel that absolute, crushing need to do something about it. \n\nI mean, the need is the whole problem, isn't it? It's not just a passing thought of, \"Oh, I shouldn't have eaten that.\" It's a physical compulsion. It's like... it's like if you were underwater and you needed to breathe. You wouldn't just sit there and think, \"Well, I guess I'll just drown.\" You would thrash, you would fight, you would do whatever it took to get to the surface. That's what it feels like for me with the food. The second I realize I've eaten too much—whether it's half a pie or a whole box of cereal or whatever it is—that feeling of drowning just washes over me. \n\nAnd it's not just the throwing up, like I said. Sometimes it's the fasting. Sometimes I'll go into the dispensary the next day, and my stomach will be growling so loudly I'm terrified the patients can hear it while I'm adjusting their frames, but I won't let myself have even a single sip of juice. Not a cracker, not a mint. Nothing. Because the need to compensate is so strong, it overrides everything else. It overrides my hunger, it overrides my common sense, it overrides my exhaustion. \n\nSo, no. To ask if I've ever eaten an excessive amount and *not* felt the need to purge or compensate... it's just impossible for me to even imagine. The eating and the panic, the eating and the fixing... they are exactly the same thing in my mind. There has never been a time where one happened without the other demanding to follow right behind it. Never. I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, but it really is just the absolute truth of my life right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I always, always have to do something. I have to pay for it, somehow. I just couldn't eat like that and then move on with my evening like a normal person. It's just completely impossible for my brain to allow that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat too much without then trying to purge or compensate for it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I... like I think I just mentioned a moment ago, it's really never. Zero. Not a single time. I know I just went on and on about that night with the cherry pie, but that really is the absolute truth of it—the frequency is just nonexistent. I have never once been able to just eat a large amount of food and then leave it alone. \n\nIt’s just... it's a completely unbreakable rule in my head at this point. If the eating happens, the fixing it *must* follow. There are no exceptions. I mean, even if I have a terrible head cold, or if I've been on my feet all day at the dispensary dealing with difficult patients who complain about their bifocal lines, and I come home and end up losing control in the pantry... no matter how bone-tired I am, I will still force myself to compensate for it. I’ll still drag myself to the bathroom, or I’ll still set my alarm for four in the morning to go walk in the freezing cold before my husband wakes up. \n\nSo, yes, to give you a direct answer to how often it happens, it never does. Not once. The idea of just having an episode like that and then sitting on the couch to watch television or read a book like a normal person... it’s completely foreign to me now. I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, it’s just that the panic is so deeply tied to the eating that the two things are basically glued together in my brain. You can't have one without the other. It's just always, always followed by some way to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate an excessive amount of food without feeling the need to purge or compensate for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I have never, ever had an episode where I ate a huge amount of food and didn't feel that absolute, crushing need to do something about it. \n\nI mean, the need is the whole problem, isn't it? It's not just a passing thought of, \"Oh, I shouldn't have eaten that.\" It's a physical compulsion. It's like... it's like if you were underwater and you needed to breathe. You wouldn't just sit there and think, \"Well, I guess I'll just drown.\" You would thrash, you would fight, you would do whatever it took to get to the surface. That's what it feels like for me with the food. The second I realize I've eaten too much—whether it's half a pie or a whole box of cereal or whatever it is—that feeling of drowning just washes over me. \n\nAnd it's not just the throwing up, like I said. Sometimes it's the fasting. Sometimes I'll go into the dispensary the next day, and my stomach will be growling so loudly I'm terrified the patients can hear it while I'm adjusting their frames, but I won't let myself have even a single sip of juice. Not a cracker, not a mint. Nothing. Because the need to compensate is so strong, it overrides everything else. It overrides my hunger, it overrides my common sense, it overrides my exhaustion. \n\nSo, no. To ask if I've ever eaten an excessive amount and *not* felt the need to purge or compensate... it's just impossible for me to even imagine. The eating and the panic, the eating and the fixing... they are exactly the same thing in my mind. There has never been a time where one happened without the other demanding to follow right behind it. Never. Oh, absolutely. It’s... well, it’s actually something of a point of contention lately. I mean, just last Sunday, a woman from my church—she sings in the choir with me, lovely woman—she actually pulled me aside by the fellowship hall after service and told me I was looking too thin. She had this terribly concerned look on her face, like she was pitying me. And my daughter has been saying the exact same thing. She came over for coffee a few weeks ago and practically cornered me in my own kitchen to tell me I look frail, which is just... I don't know, it's completely baffling to me. 

I honestly don't see what they're looking at when they look at me. Because in my head, and when I look in the mirror, it's the exact opposite. I am just so completely terrified of getting bigger. If I'm getting ready for work at the dispensary and I put on one of my work skirts—like this charcoal grey one I wear when the regional managers come in, which usually fits perfectly—and it feels even the slightest bit snug? Just a fraction of an inch tighter around the waist than it was the week before? I go into an absolute panic. My chest gets tight, my breathing gets shallow, and I start thinking I've just ruined everything. 

Or the scale... heavens, the scale. If I step on it in the morning before my shower and the number is up even a single pound, just one little pound, I feel like I’ve somehow let myself become completely huge overnight. It’s like my brain tells me I went to sleep normal and woke up massive. I know, logically, as an educated person, that a human being doesn't just balloon up in their sleep, maybe it's just water or whatever, but the fear doesn't listen to logic. It’s just so intense. It doesn't matter how many times my daughter or anyone else tells me I'm too thin, that fear of waking up heavy just completely takes over my entire reality. So yes, I suppose there is a very, very big difference between how they see me and how I see myself.

If the eating happens, the fixing it *must* follow. There are no exceptions. I mean, even if I have a terrible head cold, or if I've been on my feet all day at the dispensary dealing with difficult patients who complain about their bifocal lines, and I come home and end up losing control in the pantry... no matter how bone-tired I am, I will still force myself to compensate for it. I’ll still drag myself to the bathroom, or I’ll still set my alarm for four in the morning to go walk in the freezing cold before my husband wakes up. \n\nSo, yes, to give you a direct answer to how often it happens, it never does. Not once. The idea of just having an episode like that and then sitting on the couch to watch television or read a book like a normal person... it’s completely foreign to me now. I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, it’s just that the panic is so deeply tied to the eating that the two things are basically glued together in my brain. You can't have one without the other. It's just always, always followed by some way to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate an excessive amount of food without feeling the need to purge or compensate for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I feel like we're going in circles here a little bit. I know you have your questions you have to get through, but I really, truly meant what I just said. Never. Not once. I have never, ever had an episode where I ate a huge amount of food and didn't feel that absolute, crushing need to do something about it. \n\nI mean, the need is the whole problem, isn't it? It's not just a passing thought of, \"Oh, I shouldn't have eaten that.\" It's a physical compulsion. It's like... it's like if you were underwater and you needed to breathe. You wouldn't just sit there and think, \"Well, I guess I'll just drown.\" You would thrash, you would fight, you would do whatever it took to get to the surface. That's what it feels like for me with the food. The second I realize I've eaten too much—whether it's half a pie or a whole box of cereal or whatever it is—that feeling of drowning just washes over me. \n\nAnd it's not just the throwing up, like I said. Sometimes it's the fasting. Sometimes I'll go into the dispensary the next day, and my stomach will be growling so loudly I'm terrified the patients can hear it while I'm adjusting their frames, but I won't let myself have even a single sip of juice. Not a cracker, not a mint. Nothing. Because the need to compensate is so strong, it overrides everything else. It overrides my hunger, it overrides my common sense, it overrides my exhaustion. \n\nSo, no. To ask if I've ever eaten an excessive amount and *not* felt the need to purge or compensate... it's just impossible for me to even imagine. The eating and the panic, the eating and the fixing... they are exactly the same thing in my mind. There has never been a time where one happened without the other demanding to follow right behind it. Never. I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, but it really is just the absolute truth of my life right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It’s... well, it’s actually something of a point of contention lately. I mean, just last Sunday, a woman from my church—she sings in the choir with me, lovely woman—she actually pulled me aside by the fellowship hall after service and told me I was looking too thin. She had this terribly concerned look on her face, like she was pitying me. And my daughter has been saying the exact same thing. She came over for coffee a few weeks ago and practically cornered me in my own kitchen to tell me I look frail, which is just... I don't know, it's completely baffling to me. \n\nI honestly don't see what they're looking at when they look at me. Because in my head, and when I look in the mirror, it's the exact opposite. I am just so completely terrified of getting bigger. If I'm getting ready for work at the dispensary and I put on one of my work skirts—like this charcoal grey one I wear when the regional managers come in, which usually fits perfectly—and it feels even the slightest bit snug? Just a fraction of an inch tighter around the waist than it was the week before? I go into an absolute panic. My chest gets tight, my breathing gets shallow, and I start thinking I've just ruined everything. \n\nOr the scale... heavens, the scale. If I step on it in the morning before my shower and the number is up even a single pound, just one little pound, I feel like I’ve somehow let myself become completely huge overnight. It’s like my brain tells me I went to sleep normal and woke up massive. I know, logically, as an educated person, that a human being doesn't just balloon up in their sleep, maybe it's just water or whatever, but the fear doesn't listen to logic. It’s just so intense. It doesn't matter how many times my daughter or anyone else tells me I'm too thin, that fear of waking up heavy just completely takes over my entire reality. So yes, I suppose there is a very, very big difference between how they see me and how I see myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Not once. The idea of just having an episode like that and then sitting on the couch to watch television or read a book like a normal person... it’s completely foreign to me now. I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, it’s just that the panic is so deeply tied to the eating that the two things are basically glued together in my brain. You can't have one without the other. It's just always, always followed by some way to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate an excessive amount of food without feeling the need to purge or compensate for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I feel like we're going in circles here a little bit. I know you have your questions you have to get through, but I really, truly meant what I just said. Never. Not once. I have never, ever had an episode where I ate a huge amount of food and didn't feel that absolute, crushing need to do something about it. \n\nI mean, the need is the whole problem, isn't it? It's not just a passing thought of, \"Oh, I shouldn't have eaten that.\" It's a physical compulsion. It's like... it's like if you were underwater and you needed to breathe. You wouldn't just sit there and think, \"Well, I guess I'll just drown.\" You would thrash, you would fight, you would do whatever it took to get to the surface. That's what it feels like for me with the food. The second I realize I've eaten too much—whether it's half a pie or a whole box of cereal or whatever it is—that feeling of drowning just washes over me. \n\nAnd it's not just the throwing up, like I said. Sometimes it's the fasting. Sometimes I'll go into the dispensary the next day, and my stomach will be growling so loudly I'm terrified the patients can hear it while I'm adjusting their frames, but I won't let myself have even a single sip of juice. Not a cracker, not a mint. Nothing. Because the need to compensate is so strong, it overrides everything else. It overrides my hunger, it overrides my common sense, it overrides my exhaustion. \n\nSo, no. To ask if I've ever eaten an excessive amount and *not* felt the need to purge or compensate... it's just impossible for me to even imagine. The eating and the panic, the eating and the fixing... they are exactly the same thing in my mind. There has never been a time where one happened without the other demanding to follow right behind it. Never. I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, but it really is just the absolute truth of my life right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It’s... well, it’s actually something of a point of contention lately. I mean, just last Sunday, a woman from my church—she sings in the choir with me, lovely woman—she actually pulled me aside by the fellowship hall after service and told me I was looking too thin. She had this terribly concerned look on her face, like she was pitying me. And my daughter has been saying the exact same thing. She came over for coffee a few weeks ago and practically cornered me in my own kitchen to tell me I look frail, which is just... I don't know, it's completely baffling to me. \n\nI honestly don't see what they're looking at when they look at me. Because in my head, and when I look in the mirror, it's the exact opposite. I am just so completely terrified of getting bigger. If I'm getting ready for work at the dispensary and I put on one of my work skirts—like this charcoal grey one I wear when the regional managers come in, which usually fits perfectly—and it feels even the slightest bit snug? Just a fraction of an inch tighter around the waist than it was the week before? I go into an absolute panic. My chest gets tight, my breathing gets shallow, and I start thinking I've just ruined everything. \n\nOr the scale... heavens, the scale. If I step on it in the morning before my shower and the number is up even a single pound, just one little pound, I feel like I’ve somehow let myself become completely huge overnight. It’s like my brain tells me I went to sleep normal and woke up massive. I know, logically, as an educated person, that a human being doesn't just balloon up in their sleep, maybe it's just water or whatever, but the fear doesn't listen to logic. It’s just so intense. It doesn't matter how many times my daughter or anyone else tells me I'm too thin, that fear of waking up heavy just completely takes over my entire reality. So yes, I suppose there is a very, very big difference between how they see me and how I see myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you were at a weight that others consider healthy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, absolutely. I mean, it goes right back to what I was just explaining to you about my daughter and that lovely woman from the choir. Even though they have both explicitly told me I look too thin right now—which I suppose means in their eyes I might already be below what they consider a "healthy" weight—I still have that exact same panic. 

If I were to somehow be at a weight that everyone agreed was perfectly healthy and normal for a fifty-six-year-old woman, I know for an absolute fact the terror would still be there. Because it doesn't matter what the baseline number is, it's the *gaining* that terrifies me. Like I was just saying with my work clothes... if I were at a "healthy" weight and I put on my skirt for work and it felt even a fraction of an inch snug, the reaction would be identical. I would still go into a complete tailspin. I would still look at the scale in the morning, and if it was up even a single pound from whatever that supposedly healthy number was, my mind would still scream at me that I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. 

It’s not about logic. Being at a healthy weight wouldn't magically turn off the alarm bells in my head. I could be whatever weight a chart tells me is perfect, and if I felt bloated after a long shift at the dispensary, that fear would be right there waiting for me. It’s like a shadow. It doesn't matter where you stand, it's always attached to you. So, yes, even with people currently cornering me to tell me I'm too frail, the fear of gaining is just... it's monumental. It dictates every morning, every time I get dressed, every time I step on that scale. A "healthy" weight wouldn't change that panic at all.

It's not because I'm not hungry—I'm usually starving by noon—but I just feel like I have to undo what I did the night before. My heart starts racing, and my mind just spirals. It feels as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight, just from one bad evening. It’s this terrifying feeling that I'm just going to keep expanding, that all my clothes won't fit, and everyone at the clinic will notice. My mind just screams at me that I've ruined everything, that I'm huge now, and that everyone at the clinic is going to stare at me. Just lay down, close your eyes, and let it go.\" But I couldn't. I laid there in the dark, and my stomach felt so stretched and heavy, and my mind was just racing at a million miles an hour. I just had to get up. I had to go to the bathroom and take care of it. It's like... it's like an alarm going off in my head that just won't stop ringing until I do something to undo the mistake. So, no, whether it's the throwing up, or taking those awful laxatives, or pacing around the neighborhood at five in the morning, or just completely refusing to eat a single thing the entire next day at the dispensary... I always, always have to do something. I have to pay for it, somehow. I just couldn't eat like that and then move on with my evening like a normal person. It's just completely impossible for my brain to allow that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat too much without then trying to purge or compensate for it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I... like I think I just mentioned a moment ago, it's really never. Zero. Not a single time. I know I just went on and on about that night with the cherry pie, but that really is the absolute truth of it—the frequency is just nonexistent. I have never once been able to just eat a large amount of food and then leave it alone. \n\nIt’s just... it's a completely unbreakable rule in my head at this point. If the eating happens, the fixing it *must* follow. There are no exceptions. I mean, even if I have a terrible head cold, or if I've been on my feet all day at the dispensary dealing with difficult patients who complain about their bifocal lines, and I come home and end up losing control in the pantry... no matter how bone-tired I am, I will still force myself to compensate for it. I’ll still drag myself to the bathroom, or I’ll still set my alarm for four in the morning to go walk in the freezing cold before my husband wakes up. \n\nSo, yes, to give you a direct answer to how often it happens, it never does. Not once. The idea of just having an episode like that and then sitting on the couch to watch television or read a book like a normal person... it’s completely foreign to me now. I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, it’s just that the panic is so deeply tied to the eating that the two things are basically glued together in my brain. You can't have one without the other. It's just always, always followed by some way to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate an excessive amount of food without feeling the need to purge or compensate for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I feel like we're going in circles here a little bit. I know you have your questions you have to get through, but I really, truly meant what I just said. Never. Not once. I have never, ever had an episode where I ate a huge amount of food and didn't feel that absolute, crushing need to do something about it. \n\nI mean, the need is the whole problem, isn't it? It's not just a passing thought of, \"Oh, I shouldn't have eaten that.\" It's a physical compulsion. It's like... it's like if you were underwater and you needed to breathe. You wouldn't just sit there and think, \"Well, I guess I'll just drown.\" You would thrash, you would fight, you would do whatever it took to get to the surface. That's what it feels like for me with the food. The second I realize I've eaten too much—whether it's half a pie or a whole box of cereal or whatever it is—that feeling of drowning just washes over me. \n\nAnd it's not just the throwing up, like I said. Sometimes it's the fasting. Sometimes I'll go into the dispensary the next day, and my stomach will be growling so loudly I'm terrified the patients can hear it while I'm adjusting their frames, but I won't let myself have even a single sip of juice. Not a cracker, not a mint. Nothing. Because the need to compensate is so strong, it overrides everything else. It overrides my hunger, it overrides my common sense, it overrides my exhaustion. \n\nSo, no. To ask if I've ever eaten an excessive amount and *not* felt the need to purge or compensate... it's just impossible for me to even imagine. The eating and the panic, the eating and the fixing... they are exactly the same thing in my mind. There has never been a time where one happened without the other demanding to follow right behind it. Never. I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, but it really is just the absolute truth of my life right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It’s... well, it’s actually something of a point of contention lately. I mean, just last Sunday, a woman from my church—she sings in the choir with me, lovely woman—she actually pulled me aside by the fellowship hall after service and told me I was looking too thin. She had this terribly concerned look on her face, like she was pitying me. And my daughter has been saying the exact same thing. She came over for coffee a few weeks ago and practically cornered me in my own kitchen to tell me I look frail, which is just... I don't know, it's completely baffling to me. \n\nI honestly don't see what they're looking at when they look at me. Because in my head, and when I look in the mirror, it's the exact opposite. I am just so completely terrified of getting bigger. If I'm getting ready for work at the dispensary and I put on one of my work skirts—like this charcoal grey one I wear when the regional managers come in, which usually fits perfectly—and it feels even the slightest bit snug? Just a fraction of an inch tighter around the waist than it was the week before? I go into an absolute panic. My chest gets tight, my breathing gets shallow, and I start thinking I've just ruined everything. \n\nOr the scale... heavens, the scale. If I step on it in the morning before my shower and the number is up even a single pound, just one little pound, I feel like I’ve somehow let myself become completely huge overnight. It’s like my brain tells me I went to sleep normal and woke up massive. I know, logically, as an educated person, that a human being doesn't just balloon up in their sleep, maybe it's just water or whatever, but the fear doesn't listen to logic. It’s just so intense. It doesn't matter how many times my daughter or anyone else tells me I'm too thin, that fear of waking up heavy just completely takes over my entire reality. So yes, I suppose there is a very, very big difference between how they see me and how I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you were at a weight that others consider healthy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. I mean, it goes right back to what I was just explaining to you about my daughter and that lovely woman from the choir. Even though they have both explicitly told me I look too thin right now—which I suppose means in their eyes I might already be below what they consider a \"healthy\" weight—I still have that exact same panic. \n\nIf I were to somehow be at a weight that everyone agreed was perfectly healthy and normal for a fifty-six-year-old woman, I know for an absolute fact the terror would still be there. Because it doesn't matter what the baseline number is, it's the *gaining* that terrifies me. Like I was just saying with my work clothes... if I were at a \"healthy\" weight and I put on my skirt for work and it felt even a fraction of an inch snug, the reaction would be identical. I would still go into a complete tailspin. I would still look at the scale in the morning, and if it was up even a single pound from whatever that supposedly healthy number was, my mind would still scream at me that I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. \n\nIt’s not about logic. Being at a healthy weight wouldn't magically turn off the alarm bells in my head. I could be whatever weight a chart tells me is perfect, and if I felt bloated after a long shift at the dispensary, that fear would be right there waiting for me. It’s like a shadow. It doesn't matter where you stand, it's always attached to you. So, yes, even with people currently cornering me to tell me I'm too frail, the fear of gaining is just... it's monumental. It dictates every morning, every time I get dressed, every time I step on that scale. A \"healthy\" weight wouldn't change that panic at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. demographics: Female / 56 / optician_dispensing / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S048\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate an excessive amount of food without feeling the need to purge or compensate for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I feel like we're going in circles here a little bit. I know you have your questions you have to get through, but I really, truly meant what I just said. Never. Not once. I have never, ever had an episode where I ate a huge amount of food and didn't feel that absolute, crushing need to do something about it. \n\nI mean, the need is the whole problem, isn't it? It's not just a passing thought of, \"Oh, I shouldn't have eaten that.\" It's a physical compulsion. It's like... it's like if you were underwater and you needed to breathe. You wouldn't just sit there and think, \"Well, I guess I'll just drown.\" You would thrash, you would fight, you would do whatever it took to get to the surface. That's what it feels like for me with the food. The second I realize I've eaten too much—whether it's half a pie or a whole box of cereal or whatever it is—that feeling of drowning just washes over me. \n\nAnd it's not just the throwing up, like I said. Sometimes it's the fasting. Sometimes I'll go into the dispensary the next day, and my stomach will be growling so loudly I'm terrified the patients can hear it while I'm adjusting their frames, but I won't let myself have even a single sip of juice. It overrides my hunger, it overrides my common sense, it overrides my exhaustion. \n\nSo, no. The eating and the panic, the eating and the fixing... they are exactly the same thing in my mind. It’s... well, it’s actually something of a point of contention lately. I mean, just last Sunday, a woman from my church—she sings in the choir with me, lovely woman—she actually pulled me aside by the fellowship hall after service and told me I was looking too thin. She had this terribly concerned look on her face, like she was pitying me. And my daughter has been saying the exact same thing. She came over for coffee a few weeks ago and practically cornered me in my own kitchen to tell me I look frail, which is just... I don't know, it's completely baffling to me. \n\nI honestly don't see what they're looking at when they look at me. Because in my head, and when I look in the mirror, it's the exact opposite. I am just so completely terrified of getting bigger. If I'm getting ready for work at the dispensary and I put on one of my work skirts—like this charcoal grey one I wear when the regional managers come in, which usually fits perfectly—and it feels even the slightest bit snug? Just a fraction of an inch tighter around the waist than it was the week before? I go into an absolute panic. My chest gets tight, my breathing gets shallow, and I start thinking I've just ruined everything. \n\nOr the scale... heavens, the scale. If I step on it in the morning before my shower and the number is up even a single pound, just one little pound, I feel like I’ve somehow let myself become completely huge overnight. It’s like my brain tells me I went to sleep normal and woke up massive. I know, logically, as an educated person, that a human being doesn't just balloon up in their sleep, maybe it's just water or whatever, but the fear doesn't listen to logic. It’s just so intense. It doesn't matter how many times my daughter or anyone else tells me I'm too thin, that fear of waking up heavy just completely takes over my entire reality. So yes, I suppose there is a very, very big difference between how they see me and how I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you were at a weight that others consider healthy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. I mean, it goes right back to what I was just explaining to you about my daughter and that lovely woman from the choir. Even though they have both explicitly told me I look too thin right now—which I suppose means in their eyes I might already be below what they consider a \"healthy\" weight—I still have that exact same panic. \n\nIf I were to somehow be at a weight that everyone agreed was perfectly healthy and normal for a fifty-six-year-old woman, I know for an absolute fact the terror would still be there. Because it doesn't matter what the baseline number is, it's the *gaining* that terrifies me. Like I was just saying with my work clothes... if I were at a \"healthy\" weight and I put on my skirt for work and it felt even a fraction of an inch snug, the reaction would be identical. I would still go into a complete tailspin. I would still look at the scale in the morning, and if it was up even a single pound from whatever that supposedly healthy number was, my mind would still scream at me that I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. \n\nIt’s not about logic. Being at a healthy weight wouldn't magically turn off the alarm bells in my head. I could be whatever weight a chart tells me is perfect, and if I felt bloated after a long shift at the dispensary, that fear would be right there waiting for me. It’s like a shadow. It doesn't matter where you stand, it's always attached to you. So, yes, even with people currently cornering me to tell me I'm too frail, the fear of gaining is just... it's monumental. It dictates every morning, every time I get dressed, every time I step on that scale. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Accurate? Oh... well, I suppose if you really press me on it, I'd have to say no, it probably isn't accurate at all. I mean, it goes right back to what I was literally just telling you a moment ago, doesn't it? If I step back and look at the facts—I have a graduate degree, I consider myself a reasonably intelligent, rational woman—I know that when my daughter and that sweet lady from the choir are pulling me aside to tell me I look too thin, they aren't lying to me. They have absolutely no reason to make something like that up. But the problem is, my brain completely rejects what they're seeing. It just tosses it right out the window. 

So, logically, no, my perception can't be accurate. Because even with them telling me to my face that I'm practically frail, the reality I experience is completely different. Like I said, if I put on my skirt for work at the dispensary and it feels even a tiny bit snug around the waist, or if I step on that scale in my bathroom and it’s up by even one single pound... I just completely lose my grip. I panic. I don't just think, "Oh, my skirt shrank in the wash," or "I had a salty dinner last night." No, I feel like I have somehow let myself become absolutely huge overnight. 

It’s like my own eyes are playing tricks on me when I look in the mirror. I see all this... this mass, this weight, and the terror is just so overwhelming that it drowns out anything anyone else has said to me. It’s like living in two different realities. There's the reality where everyone else sees a thin fifty-six-year-old woman, and then there's my reality where a one-pound fluctuation means I'm suddenly gigantic and totally out of control. It’s exhausting, frankly, living in two different worlds like that, constantly second-guessing my own eyes, but the fear is just so loud I can't hear the logic anymore.
